IRS — Form 1099-K (Payment Card and Third-Party Network Transactions)\",\"2026-paycheck-take-home-pay-guide\":\"# 2026 Paycheck & Take-Home Pay Calculator Guide: How Much of Your Salary You Actually Keep\\n\\nNobody opens their first pay stub expecting the number at the bottom to feel smaller than the number they were promised in the offer letter. Yet that gap — between gross salary and net, spendable income — is exactly where most people get caught off guard. Federal income tax, FICA, state income tax, and pre-tax deductions all take a bite before your paycheck ever reaches your bank account, and the size of each bite depends on where you live, how you're paid, and how you fill out your W-4.\\n\\nThis guide walks through every piece of that puzzle for the 2026 tax year, using the latest IRS brackets, Social Security wage base, and standard deduction figures. Along the way, you'll find worked examples, state-by-state comparisons, and answers to the questions people most often ask when they're trying to figure out what a job offer is actually worth.\\n\\nFor an instant calculation based on your specific situation, you can use our [free 2026 paycheck calculator](/paycheck-calculator) which covers all 50 states and is updated for OBBBA changes.\\n\\n## Table of Contents\\n\\n1. What Determines Your Take-Home Pay\\n2. 2026 Federal Income Tax Brackets\\n3. FICA Taxes: Social Security and Medicare in 2026\\n4. State Income Tax: The Variable That Changes Everything\\n5. Step-by-Step: How to Calculate Your Net Pay\\n6. Worked Example: $75,000 Salary in Three States\\n7. Pre-Tax Deductions That Lower Your Taxable Income\\n8. Overtime, Bonuses, and Supplemental Wages\\n9. Self-Employed and 1099 Workers: A Different Formula\\n10. How to Adjust Your Withholding (W-4)\\n11. Common Take-Home Pay Mistakes to Avoid\\n12. Frequently Asked Questions\\n\\n## 1. What Determines Your Take-Home Pay\\n\\nTake-home pay, also called net pay, is what's left of your gross salary after every mandatory and elected deduction is subtracted. Four categories of deductions typically apply to a W-2 employee:\\n\\n- **Federal income tax** — a progressive tax calculated using IRS brackets and your filing status\\n- **FICA taxes** — a flat-rate combination of Social Security and Medicare\\n- **State (and sometimes local) income tax** — which varies enormously depending on where you live and work\\n- **Pre-tax and post-tax deductions** — such as 401(k) contributions, health insurance premiums, and HSA contributions\\n\\nBecause state tax rules differ so widely, two people earning identical salaries can end up with take-home pay that differs by thousands of dollars a year purely based on their zip code. That's the single biggest reason a generic paycheck calculator isn't enough — you need to understand how each layer stacks on top of the others.\\n\\n## 2. 2026 Federal Income Tax Brackets\\n\\nThe IRS adjusts its tax brackets every year for inflation, and 2026 brought a slightly larger-than-usual adjustment tied to the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA), which made most 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act provisions permanent. There are still seven federal tax rates in 2026 — 10%, 12%, 22%, 24%, 32%, 35%, and 37%.\\n\\n| Rate | Single Filers | Married Filing Jointly | Head of Household |\\n|------|---------------|------------------------|--------------------|\\n| 10% | $0 – $11,925 | $0 – $23,850 | $0 – $17,000 |\\n| 12% | $11,925 – $47,750 | $23,850 – $95,500 | $17,000 – $64,850 |\\n| 22% | $47,750 – $100,525 | $95,500 – $201,050 | $64,850 – $103,350 |\\n| 24% | $100,525 – $191,950 | $201,050 – $383,900 | $103,350 – $197,300 |\\n| 32% | $191,950 – $243,725 | $383,900 – $487,450 | $197,300 – $250,500 |\\n| 35% | $243,725 – $609,350 | $487,450 – $731,200 | $250,500 – $609,350 |\\n| 37% | $609,350+ | $731,200+ | $609,350+ |\\n\\n*Source: Tax Foundation, 2026 Tax Brackets, based on IRS Revenue Procedure 2025-25. For a deeper dive, see our complete [2026 federal tax brackets guide](/federal-tax-brackets).*\\n\\nRemember that these are **marginal** rates. Only the income that falls inside a given bracket is taxed at that bracket's rate — moving into a higher bracket never reduces your take-home pay on the dollars you already earned.\\n\\n**2026 standard deduction:**\\n\\n- Single: $16,100\\n- Married Filing Jointly: $32,200\\n- Head of Household: $24,150\\n\\n## 3. FICA Taxes: Social Security and Medicare in 2026\\n\\nFICA — the Federal Insurance Contributions Act — funds Social Security and Medicare, and it's charged at a flat rate regardless of your income level.\\n\\nThe Social Security portion (OASDI) is 6.2% on earnings up to the applicable taxable maximum, while the Medicare portion (HI) is 1.45% on all earnings with no cap. Combined, that's the familiar 7.65% FICA rate you'll see withheld from every paycheck.\\n\\nFor 2026, the Social Security wage base is $184,500, an increase of $8,400 from $176,100 in 2025 — pushing the maximum Social Security tax an employee will pay for the year up to $11,439.\\n\\n**Quick FICA reference for 2026:**\\n\\n| Component | Rate | Wage Cap |\\n|-----------|------|----------|\\n| Social Security | 6.2% (employee) + 6.2% (employer) | $184,500 |\\n| Medicare | 1.45% (employee) + 1.45% (employer) | No cap |\\n| Additional Medicare Tax | 0.9% (employee only) | Applies above $200K single / $250K MFJ |\\n\\nSelf-employed individuals pay both the employee and employer share — 15.3% total — though half of that is deductible against income tax. Use our [self-employment tax calculator](/self-employment-tax-calculator) to estimate your SE tax liability.\\n\\n## 4. State Income Tax: The Variable That Changes Everything\\n\\nFederal tax and FICA are identical no matter where you live. State tax is where paychecks diverge dramatically, and it's the piece most generic calculators oversimplify.\\n\\nStates generally fall into three buckets:\\n\\n- **No state income tax:** Alaska, Florida, Nevada, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, Washington, and Wyoming.\\n- **Flat-rate states:** States like Pennsylvania (3.07%), Illinois (4.95%), Colorado (4.4%), and Michigan (4.25%).\\n- **Progressive-bracket states:** States like California (1%–13.3%), New York (4%–10.9%), and Minnesota (5.35%–9.85%).\\n\\nOur state-specific guides for [California](/california-tax-calculator), [Texas](/texas-tax-calculator), [Florida](/florida-tax-calculator), [New York](/new-york-tax-calculator), and [Illinois](/illinois-tax-calculator) break this down in detail.\\n\\n## 5. Step-by-Step: How to Calculate Your Net Pay\\n\\nHere's the general formula that every paycheck calculator, including the fanciest ones, is ultimately running behind the scenes:\\n\\n1. **Start with gross pay** — your salary or hourly wage times hours worked.\\n2. **Subtract pre-tax deductions** — 401(k) contributions, HSA/FSA contributions, and pre-tax health insurance premiums.\\n3. **Calculate federal withholding** — apply the standard deduction, then run the remainder through the 2026 tax brackets.\\n4. **Calculate FICA** — apply 6.2% Social Security (up to $184,500 cap) and 1.45% Medicare.\\n5. **Calculate state and local tax** — using your state's specific brackets, flat rate, or exemption.\\n6. **Subtract post-tax deductions** — Roth 401(k) contributions, wage garnishments, or after-tax insurance premiums.\\n7. **What's left is your net (take-home) pay.**\\n\\nDoing this by hand for every pay period is tedious, which is exactly why [online paycheck calculators](/paycheck-calculator) exist.\\n\\n## 6. Worked Example: $75,000 Salary in Three States\\n\\nTo show how dramatically location changes the outcome, here's the same $75,000 salary (single filer, standard deduction, no pre-tax deductions) run through three different states using 2026 rates.\\n\\n**Texas (no state income tax):**\\n- Federal tax: approximately $7,670\\n- FICA: $5,737.50\\n- State tax: $0\\n- **Estimated net pay: ~$61,590**\\n\\n**Illinois (4.95% flat tax):**\\n- Federal tax: approximately $7,670\\n- FICA: $5,737.50\\n- State tax: approximately $3,575\\n- **Estimated net pay: ~$58,020**\\n\\n**California (progressive, 1%–13.3%):**\\n- Federal tax: approximately $7,670\\n- FICA: $5,737.50\\n- State tax: approximately $2,640\\n- **Estimated net pay: ~$59,000**\\n\\nThe spread between the highest and lowest outcome is roughly $3,500 a year — on an identical salary.\\n\\n## 7. Pre-Tax Deductions That Lower Your Taxable Income\\n\\nOne of the most underused levers for increasing take-home pay isn't a raise — it's reducing taxable income legally through pre-tax benefits:\\n\\n- **401(k) or 403(b) contributions:** For 2026, the employee contribution limit is $23,500.\\n- **Health Savings Account (HSA):** Up to $4,300 (self) or $8,550 (family) in 2026.\\n- **Flexible Spending Account (FSA):** Up to $3,300 in 2026.\\n- **Traditional IRA contributions:** Up to $7,000, subject to income limits.\\n\\n## 8. Overtime, Bonuses, and Supplemental Wages\\n\\nBonuses are often withheld at a flat 22% federal supplemental rate. Overtime pay under OBBBA may be eligible for a new temporary federal deduction through 2028. See our [overtime tax calculator](/overtime-tax-calculator) for details.\\n\\n## 9. Self-Employed and 1099 Workers: A Different Formula\\n\\nIf you're a freelancer, gig worker, or independent contractor, your paycheck math looks different:\\n\\n- **Self-employment tax:** You owe the full 15.3% FICA equivalent yourself.\\n- **Quarterly estimated payments:** The IRS expects estimated tax payments four times a year.\\n- **Business deductions:** Home office, mileage, equipment, and health insurance can reduce taxable income.\\n\\n## 10. How to Adjust Your Withholding (W-4)\\n\\nYour Form W-4 tells your employer how much federal tax to withhold from each paycheck. Use our [IRS withholding calculator](/irs-withholding-calculator) to estimate the right withholding amount.\\n\\n## 11. Common Take-Home Pay Mistakes to Avoid\\n\\n- **Ignoring local taxes** — Cities like NYC levy their own income tax.\\n- **Comparing gross salaries across states without adjusting for tax.**\\n- **Assuming a big refund is \\\"free money.\\\"** — A large refund means too much was withheld.\\n- **Not revisiting withholding after a raise or bonus year.**\\n\\n## 12. Frequently Asked Questions\\n\\n**Is take-home pay the same as net pay?**\\nYes. \\\"Take-home pay\\\" and \\\"net pay\\\" are used interchangeably.\\n\\n**Why did my paycheck get bigger later in the year?**\\nOnce your year-to-date earnings pass the Social Security wage base ($184,500 in 2026), the 6.2% Social Security withholding stops for the rest of the year.\\n\\n**Does bonus income get taxed at a higher rate than regular pay?**\\nNot permanently. Bonuses are often withheld at a flat 22% rate, but your actual tax liability is reconciled when you file.\\n\\n**Do I pay state tax where I live or where I work?**\\nGenerally, you owe tax to the state where the work is physically performed.\\n\\n**How much should I contribute to my 401(k) to lower my tax bracket?**\\nContributing enough to capture a full employer match is almost always worthwhile.\\n\\n**Are 2026 tax brackets higher or lower than 2025?**\\nSlightly higher in dollar terms due to annual inflation indexing plus OBBBA adjustments.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Key Takeaways\\n\\nYour paycheck is the product of several independent systems — federal brackets, a flat-rate FICA system with a rising wage base, and a patchwork of state rules — all layered on top of your gross salary. Understanding each layer, rather than treating your paycheck as a black box, puts you in a much better position to negotiate offers, plan a relocation, adjust your withholding, and avoid unpleasant surprises at tax time.\\n\\n*This article is for general informational purposes and does not constitute individualized tax or financial advice. Tax laws change frequently; consult the IRS, your state's department of revenue, or a licensed tax professional for guidance specific to your circumstances.*\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Related Calculators\\n\\n- [Free 2026 Paycheck Calculator](/paycheck-calculator) — Instant take-home pay for all 50 states\\n- [2026 Federal Tax Brackets](/federal-tax-brackets) — Complete bracket reference\\n- [OBBBA Tax Calculator](/obbba-tax-calculator) — Compare 2026 taxes under OBBBA vs prior law\\n- [Self-Employment Tax Calculator](/self-employment-tax-calculator) — SE tax + quarterly estimates\\n- [IRS Withholding Calculator](/irs-withholding-calculator) — W-4 withholding guidance\\n\\n## Related Articles\\n\\n- [How Much Tax Will I Owe in 2026?](/blog/how-much-tax-will-i-owe-2026)\\n- [2026 Federal Tax Brackets Explained](/blog/2026-federal-tax-brackets-explained)\\n- [How FICA Taxes Work in 2026](/blog/how-fica-taxes-work-2026)\\n- [1099 vs W-2 Take-Home Pay Comparison](/blog/1099-vs-w2-take-home-pay-comparison-2026)\\n- [IRS W-4 Withholding Guide](/blog/irs-withholding-w4-guide-2026)\",\"take-home-pay-calculator-guide-2026\":\"# Take Home Pay Calculator 2026: How Much You Actually Keep\\n\\nYour salary is not your take-home pay. A $75,000 salary doesn't mean $75,000 in your pocket. Between federal tax, FICA, state tax, and benefits deductions, your actual take-home pay can be $10,000-$20,000 less than your gross salary. This guide explains exactly how to calculate your take-home pay, with real examples for every state.\\n\\n## Official Source\\n\\nPaycheck withholding rules are in [IRS Publication 15-T](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p15t). State income tax rates are from the [Federation of Tax Administrators](https://www.taxadmin.org/state-tax-agencies).\\n\\n## What Is Take Home Pay?\\n\\nTake-home pay (also called net pay) is the money you actually receive in your paycheck after all deductions:\\n\\n1. **Federal income tax withholding** (based on W-4)\\n2. **FICA taxes** (Social Security 6.2% + Medicare 1.45%)\\n3. **State income tax** (varies by state, 0% - 13.3%)\\n4. **Local/city tax** (some cities like NYC)\\n5. **401(k) contributions** (if applicable)\\n6. **Health insurance premiums** (if through employer)\\n7. **HSA/FSA contributions** (if applicable)\\n\\n## How to Calculate Take Home Pay\\n\\n### Step 1: Start with Gross Salary\\n\\nThis is your annual salary before any deductions.\\n\\n### Step 2: Subtract Pre-Tax Deductions\\n\\n- 401(k) contributions (e.g., 5% of $75,000 = $3,750)\\n- HSA contributions (up to $4,400 in 2026)\\n- Health insurance premiums\\n- FSA contributions\\n\\n### Step 3: Calculate FICA Taxes\\n\\n- Social Security: 6.2% of gross wages (up to $184,500 cap)\\n- Medicare: 1.45% of all wages\\n- Additional Medicare: 0.9% on wages above $200,000\\n\\n### Step 4: Calculate Federal Income Tax\\n\\nSubtract the standard deduction ($16,100 single) from your taxable income, then apply the 2026 tax brackets.\\n\\n### Step 5: Calculate State Income Tax\\n\\nEach state has its own rates:\\n- **0% (no state tax)**: Texas, Florida, Washington, Nevada, Wyoming, South Dakota, Alaska, Tennessee, New Hampshire\\n- **Flat rate**: Illinois (4.95%), Colorado (4.4%), Indiana (2.95%), Pennsylvania (3.07%), Michigan (4.25%)\\n- **Progressive**: California (1%-13.3%), New York (4%-10.9%), and others\\n\\n### Step 6: Subtract All Taxes from Gross\\n\\nTake-home pay = Gross salary - All taxes - Pre-tax deductions\\n\\n## Take Home Pay by State — $75,000 Salary Examples\\n\\n| State | State Tax | Total Tax | Take-Home Pay | Monthly |\\n|-------|----------|-----------|--------------|---------|\\n| Texas | $0 | $14,358 | $60,642 | $5,054 |\\n| Florida | $0 | $14,358 | $60,642 | $5,054 |\\n| Washington | $0 | $14,358 | $60,642 | $5,054 |\\n| Illinois | $3,713 | $18,071 | $56,929 | $4,744 |\\n| Pennsylvania | $2,303 | $16,661 | $58,339 | $4,862 |\\n| California | $6,083 | $20,441 | $54,559 | $4,547 |\\n| New York | $5,379 | $19,737 | $55,263 | $4,605 |\\n| New York City | $7,671 | $22,029 | $52,971 | $4,414 |\\n\\nThe difference between Texas (no state tax) and California (13.3% top rate) on a $75,000 salary is **$6,083 per year** — that's $507/month extra in your pocket in Texas.\\n\\n## Take Home Pay Calculator — Free, No Signup\\n\\nOur [Paycheck Calculator](/paycheck-calculator) gives you instant take-home pay estimates:\\n- Enter your salary\\n- Select your state\\n- Choose filing status\\n- Add 401(k) and HSA contributions\\n- See your exact net pay\\n\\nNo account required. No email. No data stored. Just instant results.\\n\\n## How Much Is My Take Home Pay After 401(k)?\\n\\n401(k) contributions reduce your take-home pay, but not by the full contribution amount. Because 401(k) is pre-tax, you save on federal and state taxes.\\n\\n**Example**: You earn $75,000 and contribute 5% ($3,750) to your 401(k):\\n- Your taxable income drops to $71,250\\n- Federal tax savings: ~$825 (at 22% bracket)\\n- State tax savings: ~$186 (at 4.95% Illinois rate)\\n- **Net take-home reduction**: $3,750 - $825 - $186 = **$2,739**\\n\\nSo contributing $3,750 to your 401(k) only reduces your take-home pay by $2,739. The government effectively pays 27% of your contribution.\\n\\n## How to Increase Your Take Home Pay\\n\\n### 1. Adjust Your W-4 Withholding\\n\\nIf you typically get a large tax refund, you're over-withholding. Update your W-4 to claim additional allowances or reduce withholding. This puts more money in each paycheck.\\n\\nUse our [IRS Withholding Calculator](/irs-withholding-calculator) to find your optimal W-4 settings.\\n\\n### 2. Maximize Pre-Tax Contributions\\n\\nHSA, 401(k), and FSA contributions reduce your taxable income, which reduces your tax burden. The money comes out of your paycheck, but the tax savings partially offset the reduction.\\n\\n### 3. Move to a No-Income-Tax State\\n\\nMoving from California to Texas on a $100,000 salary increases your take-home pay by approximately **$9,000-$10,000 per year** ($750-$833/month).\\n\\n### 4. Claim All Eligible Tax Credits\\n\\nTax credits directly reduce your tax bill dollar-for-dollar:\\n- Child Tax Credit: Up to $2,000 per child\\n- Earned Income Tax Credit: Up to $7,830\\n- Child and Dependent Care Credit: Up to $1,050\\n\\n### 5. File as Head of Household\\n\\nIf you're unmarried but pay more than half the cost of keeping up a home for a qualifying person, file as Head of Household for a larger standard deduction ($24,150 vs. $16,100) and lower tax rates.\\n\\n## Take Home Pay Calculator by Salary\\n\\n| Salary | Take-Home (Texas) | Take-Home (California) | Difference |\\n|--------|-------------------|----------------------|------------|\\n| $30,000 | $26,881 | $25,648 | $1,233 |\\n| $50,000 | $42,355 | $41,097 | $1,258 |\\n| $75,000 | $60,642 | $54,559 | $6,083 |\\n| $100,000 | $78,968 | $73,799 | $5,169 |\\n| $150,000 | $114,227 | $101,237 | $12,990 |\\n| $200,000 | $148,927 | $133,454 | $15,473 |\\n\\nUse our [Salary After Tax](/salary) pages for detailed breakdowns at 26 salary levels.\\n\\n## Related Calculators\\n\\n- [Paycheck Calculator](/paycheck-calculator)\\n- [Salary After Tax by State](/salary)\\n- [401(k) Retirement Calculator](/401k-retirement-calculator)\\n- [IRS Withholding Calculator](/irs-withholding-calculator)\\n- [Relocation Calculator](/relocation-calculator)\",\"spreadsheet-formula-to-calculate-income-tax-2026\":\"# Spreadsheet Formula to Calculate Income Tax (Excel & Google Sheets)\\n\\nBuilding your own income tax calculator in a spreadsheet gives you complete control over the calculation and helps you understand exactly how progressive taxation works. Whether you use Excel or Google Sheets, this guide provides ready-to-use formulas for calculating 2026 federal income tax using the progressive bracket system.\\n\\n## How Progressive Tax Calculation Works in a Spreadsheet\\n\\nThe U.S. uses a **progressive tax system** — each portion of income is taxed at its own rate. In a spreadsheet, you calculate this by applying each bracket's rate only to the income that falls within that bracket, then summing the results.\\n\\n### The Concept\\n\\nFor a single filer with $75,000 taxable income in 2026:\\n\\n| Bracket | Rate | Income in Bracket | Tax |\\n|---------|------|-------------------|-----|\\n| $0 – $11,925 | 10% | $11,925 | $1,192.50 |\\n| $11,926 – $47,750 | 12% | $35,825 | $4,299.00 |\\n| $47,751 – $100,525 | 22% | $27,250 | $5,995.00 |\\n| **Total** | | **$75,000** | **$11,486.50** |\\n\\n## Method 1: SUMPRODUCT Formula (Easiest)\\n\\nThe SUMPRODUCT formula is the most elegant way to calculate progressive tax in a spreadsheet. It requires a bracket table and a single formula.\\n\\n### Step 1: Set Up the Bracket Table\\n\\nIn cells A1:C8, enter the 2026 single filer brackets:\\n\\n```\\nA1: Bracket Floor    B1: Bracket Ceiling    C1: Rate\\nA2: 0                B2: 11925              C2: 0.10\\nA3: 11925            B3: 47750              C3: 0.12\\nA4: 47750            B4: 100525             C4: 0.22\\nA5: 100525           B5: 191950             C5: 0.24\\nA6: 191950           B6: 243725             C6: 0.32\\nA7: 243725           B7: 609350             C7: 0.35\\nA8: 609350           B8: 9999999            C8: 0.37\\n```\\n\\n### Step 2: Enter Your Taxable Income\\n\\n```\\nE1: Taxable Income:    F1: 75000\\n```\\n\\n### Step 3: The SUMPRODUCT Formula\\n\\nIn cell G1, enter:\\n\\n```\\n=SUMPRODUCT((F1>A2:A8)*(A2:A8-A1:A7)*(C2:C8-C1:C7))\\n```\\n\\n**Wait — this formula is complex.** Let me give you a simpler approach.\\n\\n## Method 2: Nested IF Formula (Most Common)\\n\\nThis method uses nested IF statements to calculate tax for each bracket. It's easier to understand and modify.\\n\\n### For Single Filers (2026 Brackets)\\n\\nAssume your taxable income is in cell **A1**:\\n\\n```\\n=IF(A1<=11925, A1*0.10,\\n  IF(A1<=47750, 1192.50+(A1-11925)*0.12,\\n    IF(A1<=100525, 5511+(A1-47750)*0.22,\\n      IF(A1<=191950, 16065.50+(A1-100525)*0.24,\\n        IF(A1<=243725, 37933.50+(A1-191950)*0.32,\\n          IF(A1<=609350, 54541.50+(A1-243725)*0.35,\\n            182757+(A1-609350)*0.37\\n          )\\n        )\\n      )\\n    )\\n  )\\n)\\n```\\n\\n### How This Formula Works\\n\\nEach IF statement checks if income falls within a bracket:\\n1. If income ≤ $11,925 → tax = income × 10%\\n2. If income ≤ $47,750 → tax = $1,192.50 + (income − $11,925) × 12%\\n3. If income ≤ $100,525 → tax = $5,511 + (income − $47,750) × 22%\\n4. And so on...\\n\\nThe formula adds the cumulative tax from lower brackets plus the tax on the portion within the current bracket.\\n\\n## Method 3: VLOOKUP Formula (Most Maintainable)\\n\\nThis method uses a lookup table, making it easy to update when brackets change.\\n\\n### Step 1: Create the Tax Table\\n\\nIn cells A1:C7, enter:\\n\\n```\\nA1: Lower Bound    B1: Base Tax    C1: Rate\\nA2: 0              B2: 0           C2: 0.10\\nA3: 11925          B3: 1192.50     C3: 0.12\\nA4: 47750          B4: 5511        C4: 0.22\\nA5: 100525         B5: 16065.50    C5: 0.24\\nA6: 191950         B6: 37933.50    C6: 0.32\\nA7: 243725         B7: 54541.50    C7: 0.35\\nA8: 609350         B8: 182757      C8: 0.37\\n```\\n\\n### Step 2: The VLOOKUP Formula\\n\\nAssume taxable income is in cell **E1**:\\n\\n```\\n=VLOOKUP(E1, A2:C8, 2, TRUE) + (E1 - VLOOKUP(E1, A2:C8, 1, TRUE)) * VLOOKUP(E1, A2:C8, 3, TRUE)\\n```\\n\\n### How This Works\\n\\n1. `VLOOKUP(E1, A2:C8, 2, TRUE)` — Finds the base tax for your bracket\\n2. `VLOOKUP(E1, A2:C8, 1, TRUE)` — Finds the lower bound of your bracket\\n3. `VLOOKUP(E1, A2:C8, 3, TRUE)` — Finds the rate for your bracket\\n4. The formula: Base Tax + (Income − Lower Bound) × Rate = Total Tax\\n\\n## Adding the Standard Deduction\\n\\nTo calculate taxable income from gross income, subtract the 2026 standard deduction:\\n\\n### Single Filers\\n```\\nTaxable Income = Gross Income - 16100\\n```\\n\\n### Married Filing Jointly\\n```\\nTaxable Income = Gross Income - 32200\\n```\\n\\n### Complete Spreadsheet Formula (Single Filer)\\n\\nIf your gross income is in cell **A1**:\\n\\n```\\n=IF(MAX(0,A1-16100)<=11925, MAX(0,A1-16100)*0.10,\\n  IF(MAX(0,A1-16100)<=47750, 1192.50+(MAX(0,A1-16100)-11925)*0.12,\\n    IF(MAX(0,A1-16100)<=100525, 5511+(MAX(0,A1-16100)-47750)*0.22,\\n      IF(MAX(0,A1-16100)<=191950, 16065.50+(MAX(0,A1-16100)-100525)*0.24,\\n        IF(MAX(0,A1-16100)<=243725, 37933.50+(MAX(0,A1-16100)-191950)*0.32,\\n          IF(MAX(0,A1-16100)<=609350, 54541.50+(MAX(0,A1-16100)-243725)*0.35,\\n            182757+(MAX(0,A1-16100)-609350)*0.37\\n          )\\n        )\\n      )\\n    )\\n  )\\n)\\n```\\n\\n## Married Filing Jointly Brackets (2026)\\n\\nFor MFJ, use these cumulative base taxes:\\n\\n| Lower Bound | Base Tax | Rate |\\n|-------------|---------|------|\\n| 0 | 0 | 10% |\\n| 23850 | 2385 | 12% |\\n| 95500 | 9777 | 22% |\\n| 201050 | 32998 | 24% |\\n| 383900 | 76906 | 32% |\\n| 487450 | 110133 | 35% |\\n| 731200 | 195595.50 | 37% |\\n\\n## Adding FICA Tax\\n\\nFICA (Social Security + Medicare) is separate from income tax:\\n\\n```\\nFICA = (Gross Income × 0.062, capped at 184500) + (Gross Income × 0.0145)\\n```\\n\\n### Spreadsheet Formula for FICA\\n\\nIf gross income is in cell A1:\\n\\n```\\n=MIN(A1, 184500) * 0.062 + A1 * 0.0145\\n```\\n\\n### Total Tax (Federal + FICA)\\n\\n```\\nTotal Tax = Federal Income Tax + FICA\\n```\\n\\n## Adding State Income Tax\\n\\nState tax calculation depends on your state. For flat-tax states (like Illinois at 4.95%):\\n\\n```\\nState Tax = Taxable Income × 0.0495\\n```\\n\\nFor states with no income tax (Texas, Florida, Nevada, etc.):\\n\\n```\\nState Tax = 0\\n```\\n\\nUse our state tax calculators (e.g., [Texas](/texas-tax-calculator), [California](/california-tax-calculator)) for state-specific calculations.\\n\\n## Complete Take-Home Pay Calculator Spreadsheet\\n\\nHere's a summary of all formulas needed:\\n\\n| Cell | Label | Formula |\\n|------|-------|---------|\\n| A1 | Gross Income | (user input) |\\n| A2 | 401(k) Contribution | (user input) |\\n| A3 | Filing Status | (user input) |\\n| A4 | Taxable Income | =MAX(0, A1-A2-IF(A3=\\\"Single\\\",16100,32200)) |\\n| A5 | Federal Income Tax | (nested IF formula from above) |\\n| A6 | FICA Tax | =MIN(A1,184500)*0.062+A1*0.0145 |\\n| A7 | Total Tax | =A5+A6 |\\n| A8 | Take-Home Pay | =A1-A2-A7 |\\n| A9 | Effective Tax Rate | =A5/A1 |\\n| A10 | Marginal Tax Rate | (VLOOKUP on bracket table) |\\n\\n## Google Sheets vs Excel\\n\\nBoth platforms support all formulas in this guide. Key differences:\\n\\n| Feature | Google Sheets | Excel |\\n|---------|--------------|-------|\\n| IFS function | ✅ Available | ✅ Excel 2019+ |\\n| VLOOKUP | ✅ | ✅ |\\n| SUMPRODUCT | ✅ | ✅ |\\n| Array formulas | Auto | Ctrl+Shift+Enter |\\n| Price | Free | $139+ |\\n\\n## Frequently Asked Questions\\n\\n**Can I use these formulas for state income tax?**\\nYes, but you need to use your state's specific brackets and rates. Flat-tax states (Illinois, Colorado, etc.) use a simple multiplication. States with progressive brackets (California, New York) need the same nested IF or VLOOKUP approach.\\n\\n**How do I account for tax credits in the spreadsheet?**\\nSubtract credits after calculating the tax. For example, the Child Tax Credit: `=Federal Tax - (Number_of_Children × 2200)`\\n\\n**Do these formulas include the standard deduction?**\\nThe complete formula (Method 2 with MAX) includes the standard deduction. The bracket-only formulas calculate tax on taxable income (after deductions).\\n\\n**How accurate are these formulas?**\\nThey match the IRS calculation method exactly. However, real-world taxes include additional factors (AMT, self-employment tax, capital gains rates) that may require additional formulas.\\n\\n**Can I download a template?**\\nUse our [paycheck calculator](/paycheck-calculator) for instant results. For a spreadsheet version, copy the formulas above into Excel or Google Sheets.\\n\\n## Related Resources\\n\\n- [Paycheck Calculator](/paycheck-calculator) — Instant federal + state tax calculation\\n- [2026 Tax Brackets](/blog/2026-federal-tax-brackets-explained) — Complete bracket reference\\n- [OASDI Tax Explained](/blog/oasdi-tax-explained-2026) — Social Security tax details\\n- [OBBBA Tax Calculator](/obbba-tax-calculator) — 2026 tax law comparison\\n\\n## Sources\\n\\n1. IRS — Revenue Procedure 2025-25 (2026 Tax Brackets)\\n2. IRS — Publication 505 (Tax Withholding and Estimated Tax)\\n3. Microsoft Excel Documentation — VLOOKUP and IF functions\\n4. Google Sheets Documentation — Array formulas and SUMPRODUCT\",\"sep-ira-solo-401k-guide-2026\":\"# SEP IRA vs Solo 401k: Which Is Better for Self-Employed in 2026?\\n\\nSarah is a freelance graphic designer who made $80,000 last year. She's been self-employed for three years, and every tax season she watches a huge chunk of her income disappear to taxes. Her accountant mentioned she should open a retirement plan — something that could cut her tax bill AND help her save for the future. But when Sarah started researching, she got lost in the alphabet soup: SEP IRA, Solo 401k, SIMPLE IRA, Traditional IRA, Roth IRA... which one actually makes sense?\\n\\nIf you're self-employed — whether you're a freelancer, contractor, consultant, gig worker, or small business owner — you've probably hit the same wall. You want to reduce your tax bill. You want to save for retirement. But the options are confusing, and the wrong choice could mean leaving thousands of dollars on the table every single year.\\n\\nTwo plans dominate the conversation for self-employed workers: the **SEP IRA** and the **Solo 401k**. Both offer serious tax benefits. Both let you contribute far more than a regular IRA. But they work very differently, and the \\\"best\\\" one depends entirely on your situation.\\n\\nIn this guide, I'll break down the SEP IRA contribution limits for 2026, explain how a Solo 401k works, compare them side by side, and show you real numbers at different income levels so you can make the right call. Let's dive in.\\n\\n## What Is a SEP IRA?\\n\\nSEP stands for Simplified Employee Pension. Despite the fancy name, it's actually one of the simplest retirement plans available for self-employed people and small business owners.\\n\\nA SEP IRA works like this: you (the employer) contribute to a traditional IRA set up in your name (the employee's). There's no employee contribution — the employer puts in all the money. And since you're self-employed, you're both the employer AND the employee.\\n\\n### SEP IRA Contribution Limits for 2026\\n\\nFor 2026, the SEP IRA contribution limit is **25% of your net self-employment income, up to a maximum of $69,000**.\\n\\nBut here's the catch that trips people up: \\\"net self-employment income\\\" isn't your gross revenue. It's your profit after expenses, minus half of your self-employment tax, and further reduced by the SEP contribution itself. The actual formula is a bit circular, which is why the effective contribution rate works out to roughly **20% of your net profit** (after the SE tax deduction), not 25%.\\n\\nLet me show you the math. Say your Schedule C net profit is $80,000:\\n\\n1. Subtract half of SE tax: $80,000 − $5,652 = $74,348 (your \\\"compensation\\\")\\n2. 25% of compensation: $74,348 × 0.25 = $18,587\\n3. Your SEP IRA contribution: **$18,587**\\n\\nWait — that's only 23.2% of your original $80,000. Where'd the 25% go? The IRS formula accounts for the fact that the contribution itself reduces your compensation. It's a chicken-and-egg problem that the IRS resolves with a specific calculation. Our [self-employment tax calculator](/self-employment-tax-calculator) handles this automatically if you want exact numbers.\\n\\n### Who Can Open a SEP IRA?\\n\\n- Self-employed individuals (freelancers, contractors, sole proprietors, LLC owners)\\n- Small business owners with employees\\n- Anyone with earned income from self-employment, even if you also have a W-2 job\\n- You can set one up even if you're a side hustler with a full-time job\\n\\n### Key SEP IRA Features\\n\\n**Tax deduction**: Every dollar you contribute to a SEP IRA is tax-deductible. That means a $10,000 contribution reduces your taxable income by $10,000. If you're in the 22% federal bracket, that's $2,200 less in federal taxes alone.\\n\\n**Deadline**: You can open and fund a SEP IRA up until the tax filing deadline, **including extensions**. That means you could open one as late as October 15, 2027 for the 2026 tax year (if you file an extension). This is a huge advantage if you're a procrastinator — or if you want to see your full-year numbers before deciding how much to contribute.\\n\\n**No catch-up contributions**: Unlike some other plans, SEP IRAs don't offer extra contributions for people 50 and older. The limit is the limit, period.\\n\\n**Employer-only contributions**: The business makes all contributions. There's no \\\"employee deferral\\\" like you'd find in a 401k. This is important because it limits how much you can contribute at lower income levels.\\n\\n**Employee coverage requirement**: If you have employees, you generally must contribute to SEP IRAs for all eligible employees at the same percentage rate you contribute for yourself. This can get expensive quickly.\\n\\n**Tax-deferred growth**: Investments grow tax-deferred inside the SEP IRA. You pay taxes when you withdraw in retirement.\\n\\n## What Is a Solo 401k?\\n\\nA Solo 401k — also called an Individual 401k or One-Participant 401k — is designed specifically for self-employed people with **no employees** (other than a spouse). It lets you wear two hats: you're both the employer AND the employee, and you can contribute in both capacities.\\n\\nThis dual-contribution structure is what makes the Solo 401k so powerful, especially at lower and middle income levels.\\n\\n### Solo 401k Contribution Limits for 2026\\n\\nFor 2026, the Solo 401k contribution limit is **up to $69,000 total** — but you get there through two separate channels:\\n\\n1. **Employee contribution (elective deferral)**: Up to **$23,000** of your compensation. This is money you choose to defer from your pay into the 401k.\\n2. **Catch-up contribution (age 50+)**: An additional **$7,500** on top of the $23,000 employee deferral, bringing the employee side to $30,500 if you're 50 or older.\\n3. **Employer contribution**: Up to **25% of your compensation** (same calculation as SEP IRA).\\n4. **Total maximum**: $69,000 for 2026 (or $76,500 if you're 50+ with catch-up).\\n\\nThe magic of the Solo 401k is that the employee contribution doesn't depend on a percentage of income. It's a flat dollar amount — $23,000 — as long as you earn at least that much. So even if your net SE income is relatively modest, you can still stash away a significant amount.\\n\\n### Key Solo 401k Features\\n\\n**Roth option**: Most Solo 401k plans allow a designated Roth account. This means you can make after-tax contributions that grow tax-free and can be withdrawn tax-free in retirement. With a SEP IRA, there's no Roth option — everything is pre-tax.\\n\\n**Loan option**: Solo 401k plans can allow loans of up to **$50,000 or 50% of your vested balance**, whichever is less. You pay yourself back with interest. This can be a lifeline if you need access to cash without triggering early withdrawal penalties. SEP IRAs don't offer loans.\\n\\n**Deadline**: A Solo 401k must be established by **December 31** of the tax year. You can make contributions up until the tax filing deadline (including extensions), but the plan itself must exist before the year ends. This is a crucial difference from the SEP IRA.\\n\\n**Annual filing requirement**: If your Solo 401k balance exceeds **$250,000**, you must file Form 5500-EZ annually. It's a relatively simple form, but it's one more thing on your to-do list. SEP IRAs have no annual filing requirements regardless of balance.\\n\\n**No employees allowed**: You cannot have any full-time employees (working 1,000+ hours per year) other than yourself and your spouse. If you hire an employee, you'd need to convert to a regular 401k plan or switch to a SEP IRA.\\n\\n**Cost**: Solo 401k plans typically cost $100–500 per year to maintain, depending on the provider and whether you want Roth and loan features. Some providers offer free basic plans. SEP IRAs are usually free or very low cost ($0–50/year).\\n\\n## SEP IRA vs Solo 401k: Side-by-Side Comparison\\n\\nLet's put them head to head. Here's the complete comparison:\\n\\n| Feature | SEP IRA | Solo 401k |\\n|---------|---------|-----------|\\n| Max contribution (2026) | $69,000 | $69,000 ($76,500 if 50+) |\\n| Employee contributions | No | Yes (up to $23,000) |\\n| Roth option | No | Yes |\\n| Loans allowed | No | Yes (up to $50,000) |\\n| Catch-up contributions (50+) | No | Yes ($7,500) |\\n| Setup complexity | Easy | Moderate |\\n| Annual filing required | No | Yes (if balance > $250k) |\\n| Employees allowed | Yes (with required contributions) | No (self + spouse only) |\\n| Setup deadline | Tax filing deadline (incl. extensions) | December 31 of tax year |\\n| Contribution deadline | Tax filing deadline (incl. extensions) | Tax filing deadline (incl. extensions) |\\n| Cost to maintain | Low ($0–50/year) | Moderate ($100–500/year) |\\n| Tax deduction | Full contribution deductible | Pre-tax portion deductible |\\n| Early withdrawal penalty | 10% before age 59½ | 10% before age 59½ (loan exception available) |\\n\\n## Contribution Examples by Income Level\\n\\nThis is where the rubber meets the road. The maximum contribution is the same for both plans ($69,000), but **how much you can actually contribute at different income levels varies significantly**. Let's run the numbers.\\n\\n*Note: For these calculations, I'm using net self-employment profit (Schedule C income after expenses). The Solo 401k calculations include the $23,000 employee deferral plus the employer contribution of 25% of adjusted compensation. The SEP IRA is 25% of adjusted compensation (roughly 20% of net profit).*\\n\\n### $30,000 Net SE Income\\n\\n**SEP IRA:**\\n- Adjusted compensation: ~$27,713\\n- 25% contribution: **$6,928**\\n\\n**Solo 401k:**\\n- Employee deferral: $23,000 (limited to 100% of compensation, which is ~$27,713 — so full $23,000 allowed)\\n- Employer contribution (25% of compensation): ~$6,928\\n- Wait — you can't contribute more than your net earnings. The total can't exceed your net SE income.\\n- Actual total: **$27,713** (capped at earnings)\\n\\nBut realistically, most providers limit employee deferrals to your net earnings. Let me simplify:\\n- Employee deferral: $23,000\\n- Employer contribution: ~$1,755 (25% of compensation after employee deferral)\\n- **Total Solo 401k: ~$24,755**\\n\\n**Winner: Solo 401k** by a mile. At $30k income, the Solo 401k lets you contribute roughly $24,755 vs only $6,928 with a SEP IRA. That's nearly 4x more.\\n\\n### $50,000 Net SE Income\\n\\n**SEP IRA:**\\n- Adjusted compensation: ~$46,426\\n- 25% contribution: **$11,607**\\n\\n**Solo 401k:**\\n- Employee deferral: $23,000\\n- Employer contribution: ~$5,856\\n- **Total Solo 401k: ~$28,856**\\n\\n**Winner: Solo 401k** again. $28,856 vs $11,607 — you can save an extra $17,249 per year.\\n\\n### $80,000 Net SE Income\\n\\n**SEP IRA:**\\n- Adjusted compensation: ~$74,348\\n- 25% contribution: **$18,587**\\n\\n**Solo 401k:**\\n- Employee deferral: $23,000\\n- Employer contribution: ~$12,837\\n- **Total Solo 401k: ~$35,837**\\n\\n**Winner: Solo 401k** still. At $80k, the Solo 401k lets you put away nearly double what the SEP IRA allows.\\n\\n### $120,000 Net SE Income\\n\\n**SEP IRA:**\\n- Adjusted compensation: ~$111,522\\n- 25% contribution: **$27,880**\\n\\n**Solo 401k:**\\n- Employee deferral: $23,000\\n- Employer contribution: ~$22,130\\n- **Total Solo 401k: ~$45,130**\\n\\n**Winner: Solo 401k** — but the gap is narrowing. $45,130 vs $27,880. The SEP IRA percentage-based contribution is catching up.\\n\\n### $200,000 Net SE Income\\n\\n**SEP IRA:**\\n- Adjusted compensation: ~$185,870\\n- 25% contribution: **$46,468**\\n\\n**Solo 401k:**\\n- Employee deferral: $23,000\\n- Employer contribution: ~$40,717\\n- **Total Solo 401k: ~$63,717**\\n\\n**Winner: Solo 401k** — but barely. At $200k, both plans are approaching the $69,000 cap. Above roughly $280,000 in net SE income, both plans max out at $69,000 and the difference becomes negligible.\\n\\n**The key takeaway**: If your self-employment income is under ~$280,000, the Solo 401k almost always lets you contribute more. The lower your income, the bigger the advantage. At higher incomes, the two plans converge.\\n\\n## When SEP IRA Wins\\n\\nThe Solo 401k isn't always the right choice. Here are the situations where a SEP IRA makes more sense:\\n\\n### You Have Employees\\n\\nIf your business has eligible employees (anyone 21+ who worked for you in 3 of the last 5 years and earned $750+), you **must** contribute to their SEP IRAs at the same rate you contribute to yours. While that sounds expensive, it's actually the reason the SEP IRA exists — it was designed for small businesses with employees.\\n\\nA Solo 401k, on the other hand, can't have any employees (other than your spouse). So if you have even one full-time employee, the Solo 401k is off the table unless you want to convert to a full 401k plan (much more complex and expensive).\\n\\n### You Want Simplicity and Low Cost\\n\\nA SEP IRA is dead simple. You fill out Form 5305-SEP (one page), open an account with any brokerage, and you're done. No annual filings. No complex administration. Maintenance costs are usually zero.\\n\\nA Solo 401k requires more paperwork to set up, may have annual fees, and if your balance exceeds $250,000, you need to file Form 5500-EZ every year. For some people, that extra complexity isn't worth it.\\n\\n### You're a Side Hustler with W-2 Income\\n\\nIf you have a full-time W-2 job that already offers a 401k, and your self-employment income is from a side gig, a SEP IRA might be the cleaner option. Here's why: your 401k employee deferral limit ($23,000 for 2026) is shared across ALL 401k plans. If you're already maxing out your W-2 401k, you can't make employee deferrals into a Solo 401k too.\\n\\nWith a SEP IRA, there's no employee deferral — it's all employer contributions. So your W-2 401k contributions don't interfere. You can max out your workplace 401k AND contribute to a SEP IRA from your side business.\\n\\n### You Missed the Solo 401k Deadline\\n\\nRemember, a Solo 401k must be established by December 31 of the tax year. A SEP IRA can be opened and funded up until the tax filing deadline, including extensions. If it's January and you're looking at your 2026 taxes wishing you'd set up a retirement plan — a SEP IRA can still save you. A Solo 401k for 2026? Too late.\\n\\nThis deadline flexibility alone makes the SEP IRA worth considering as a backup option.\\n\\n## When Solo 401k Wins\\n\\nFor many self-employed people, especially freelancers and solo consultants, the Solo 401k is the better retirement plan. Here's why:\\n\\n### You Want to Contribute MORE at Lower Incomes\\n\\nThis is the #1 reason to choose a Solo 401k. As we saw in the contribution examples, the Solo 401k's employee deferral of $23,000 means you can save dramatically more at lower and middle income levels. At $50,000 of self-employment income, a Solo 401k lets you contribute roughly $28,856 vs only $11,607 with a SEP IRA.\\n\\nThat's an extra $17,249 per year going into your retirement account — and an extra $17,249 deducted from your taxable income. Use our [401(k) calculator](/401k-retirement-calculator) to see how that extra contribution compounds over 20 or 30 years. Spoiler: it's hundreds of thousands of dollars.\\n\\n### You Want a Roth Option\\n\\nThe Solo 401k's Roth feature is a big deal. You can split your contributions between pre-tax and Roth, giving you tax diversification in retirement. With a SEP IRA, everything is pre-tax — you'll pay ordinary income tax on every dollar you withdraw.\\n\\nIf you think tax rates might be higher in the future (and with the national debt where it is, that's a reasonable bet), having some Roth money gives you a tax-free bucket to draw from. This flexibility is invaluable in retirement planning.\\n\\nYou can also roll a SEP IRA into a Solo 401k later if you want Roth access, but that requires opening and maintaining the Solo 401k first.\\n\\n### You Want Loan Access\\n\\nLife happens. Sometimes you need cash and you don't want to pay the 10% early withdrawal penalty. A Solo 401k lets you borrow up to $50,000 (or 50% of your vested balance, whichever is less) and pay yourself back with interest.\\n\\nIs borrowing from your retirement ideal? No. But it's a lot better than taking a hardship withdrawal or racking up credit card debt at 25% interest. With a SEP IRA, there's no loan option — if you need money before 59½, you're taking a distribution and paying taxes plus the 10% penalty.\\n\\n### You're Over 50 and Want Catch-Up Contributions\\n\\nIf you're 50 or older, the Solo 401k lets you contribute an extra $7,500 as a catch-up contribution. That brings your employee deferral to $30,500 and your total possible contribution to $76,500. A SEP IRA has no catch-up provision — the $69,000 cap is the same whether you're 25 or 65.\\n\\nIf you're getting a late start on retirement savings (and many self-employed people are), those catch-up contributions can make a real difference.\\n\\n### Your Income Is Under $69,000\\n\\nAt incomes below roughly $69,000, the Solo 401k advantage is massive. You can potentially contribute nearly all of your net earnings, while the SEP IRA limits you to about 20%. If you're a freelancer earning $40,000 and want to save aggressively, the Solo 401k is the only plan that lets you put away a meaningful amount.\\n\\n## The Tax Savings Calculator: Real Numbers\\n\\nLet's see the actual tax impact. We'll use Sarah, our freelance graphic designer earning $80,000 in net SE income, as an example. She's single, takes the standard deduction, and lives in a state with 5% income tax.\\n\\n### Without Any Retirement Plan\\n\\n- Net SE income: $80,000\\n- Self-employment tax: ~$11,304 (15.3% × 92.35% × $80,000)\\n- Half of SE tax deduction: $5,652\\n- Adjusted gross income: ~$74,348\\n- Standard deduction: $15,200\\n- Taxable income: ~$59,148\\n- Federal income tax: ~$8,576\\n- State tax (5%): ~$3,717\\n- **Total tax: ~$23,597**\\n\\n### With SEP IRA ($18,587 contribution)\\n\\n- SEP IRA deduction: $18,587\\n- New adjusted gross income: ~$55,761\\n- Taxable income: ~$40,561\\n- Federal income tax: ~$5,337\\n- State tax (5%): ~$2,788\\n- Self-employment tax: ~$11,304 (SEP doesn't reduce SE tax)\\n- **Total tax: ~$19,429**\\n- **Tax savings: ~$4,168**\\n- Plus $18,587 going into your retirement account\\n\\n### With Solo 401k ($35,837 contribution)\\n\\n- Solo 401k deduction: $35,837\\n- New adjusted gross income: ~$38,511\\n- Taxable income: ~$23,311\\n- Federal income tax: ~$2,597\\n- State tax (5%): ~$1,926\\n- Self-employment tax: ~$11,304\\n- **Total tax: ~$15,827**\\n- **Tax savings: ~$7,770**\\n- Plus $35,837 going into your retirement account\\n\\n**The Solo 401k saves Sarah an additional $3,602 in taxes compared to the SEP IRA**, AND she puts $17,250 more into her retirement account. That's a double win.\\n\\nOur [paycheck calculator](/paycheck-calculator) can help you model your own numbers, and if you have investment income on top of self-employment income, check the [capital gains calculator](/capital-gains-calculator) for the full picture.\\n\\n### Impact on Self-Employment Tax\\n\\nOne important note: neither a SEP IRA nor a Solo 401k reduces your self-employment tax. The 15.3% SE tax is calculated on your net earnings **before** any retirement contributions. Retirement deductions only reduce your income tax, not your SE tax.\\n\\nThe only things that reduce SE tax are business expense deductions (which reduce your net profit) and the above-the-line deduction for half of SE tax itself.\\n\\n## SIMPLE IRA: The Third Option\\n\\nBefore we wrap up, there's one more plan worth mentioning: the **SIMPLE IRA**. It's less common for sole proprietors, but it fills a niche.\\n\\n### SIMPLE IRA Contribution Limits for 2026\\n\\n- Employee contribution: up to **$16,000**\\n- Catch-up contribution (50+): additional **$3,500**\\n- Employer match: dollar-for-dollar up to 3% of compensation, OR 2% nonelective contribution\\n\\n### When a SIMPLE IRA Makes Sense\\n\\n- You have a small number of employees and want a low-cost plan\\n- You want both employer and employee contributions but find the Solo 401k too complex\\n- Your employees earn modest wages and a 2-3% match is affordable\\n\\n### Why It's Usually Not the Best for Solo Self-Employed\\n\\nFor a solo self-employed person, the SIMPLE IRA almost always allows less in total contributions than either a SEP IRA or Solo 401k. The $16,000 employee limit plus a 3% match comes out to significantly less than what you can contribute to a Solo 401k. And unlike a SEP IRA, a SIMPLE IRA requires employer contributions.\\n\\nThe SIMPLE IRA is best suited for very small businesses (1-10 employees) where the owner wants to offer a retirement benefit but can't afford or manage a full 401k plan.\\n\\n## How to Open Each Plan\\n\\n### Opening a SEP IRA\\n\\n1. **Choose a brokerage** — Fidelity, Vanguard, Schwab, and E*TRADE all offer fee-free SEP IRAs\\n2. **Fill out Form 5305-SEP** — This is the plan adoption agreement. Some brokerages have their own version\\n3. **Open the account** — Usually takes 10-15 minutes online\\n4. **Fund it** — Transfer money from your bank account or business account\\n5. **Invest** — Choose your investments (index funds, target-date funds, etc.)\\n\\nThat's it. No IRS filing required. No annual forms. The whole process takes less than 30 minutes.\\n\\n### Opening a Solo 401k\\n\\n1. **Choose a provider** — Look for one that offers Roth and loan features if you want them. Popular options include Fidelity, Vanguard, Schwab, E*TRADE, and specialized providers like Nabers Group and MySolo401k\\n2. **Adopt the plan** — You'll need to sign a plan adoption agreement and a trust document. Some providers make this simpler than others\\n3. **Open the account** — You may need both a pre-tax and a Roth sub-account\\n4. **Get an EIN** — You need an Employer Identification Number from the IRS (free, takes 5 minutes online at irs.gov) even if you have no employees\\n5. **Fund it** — Make employee deferrals and employer contributions separately\\n6. **Invest** — Choose your investments within the plan\\n\\nThe setup is more involved than a SEP IRA, but most providers walk you through it. Expect to spend 1-2 hours getting everything set up.\\n\\n**Pro tip**: If you want the Roth option and loan feature, make sure your provider offers them before opening the account. Not all Solo 401k providers do — the free plans from major brokerages often lack these features.\\n\\n## People Also Ask\\n\\n### What are the SEP IRA contribution limits for 2026?\\n\\nFor 2026, the SEP IRA contribution limit is 25% of your net self-employment income, up to a maximum of $69,000. In practice, because of how the IRS calculates compensation, your actual contribution works out to roughly 20% of your net Schedule C profit. If you earn $100,000 in net self-employment income, you can contribute approximately $18,587 to a SEP IRA for 2026.\\n\\n### Can I have both a SEP IRA and a Solo 401k?\\n\\nYes, you can have both, but the total employer contribution across both plans cannot exceed 25% of your compensation, and the $69,000 annual addition limit applies across both plans combined. In most cases, it's simpler and more effective to choose one plan. Some people open a SEP IRA for years when they had employees, then roll it into a Solo 401k after closing their business to employees.\\n\\n### Is a Solo 401k better than a SEP IRA?\\n\\nFor most self-employed people without employees, yes — a Solo 401k is better than a SEP IRA. The Solo 401k allows higher contributions at most income levels, offers a Roth option, permits loans, and includes catch-up contributions for those 50+. The SEP IRA wins if you have employees, want maximum simplicity, need the flexible deadline, or are already maxing out a W-2 401k at your day job.\\n\\n### How much can a self-employed person contribute to a SEP IRA?\\n\\nA self-employed person can contribute up to 25% of their net self-employment income to a SEP IRA, capped at $69,000 for 2026. Because of the IRS calculation method, the effective contribution rate is approximately 20% of your net profit. For example, on $80,000 of self-employment income, you could contribute about $18,587. You can contribute to a SEP IRA even if you also participate in a retirement plan through a W-2 employer.\\n\\n---\\n\\nChoosing between a SEP IRA and a Solo 401k isn't about which plan is \\\"better\\\" in the abstract — it's about which one is better for **you**. If you're a solo operator earning $50k-$150k from self-employment, the Solo 401k almost always lets you contribute more, save more on taxes, and gives you flexibility with Roth and loan options. If you have employees, value simplicity above all else, or need the extended deadline, the SEP IRA is your friend.\\n\\nEither way, the most important thing is to actually open one. Too many self-employed people pay thousands in extra taxes every year because they never set up a retirement plan. Don't be one of them. Pick a plan, open it this week, and start saving. Your future self — and your tax return — will thank you.\",\"tax-questions-answered-2026\":\"# Tax Questions Answered: Brackets, Overtime, Bonuses & More (2026)\\n\\nAmericans have hundreds of questions about how taxes work. From brackets to overtime pay to bonuses, the tax code is complex and confusing. This FAQ guide answers the most commonly searched tax questions for 2026, with clear, accurate explanations.\\n\\n## Official Source\\n\\nOvertime and bonus tax rules are in [IRS Publication 15-T](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p15t). State tax comparisons are from the [Tax Foundation](https://taxfoundation.org/).\\n\\n## Are No Taxes on Overtime in Effect?\\n\\nAs of 2026, there is **no federal exemption** for overtime pay. Overtime pay is still taxed as ordinary income at the federal level.\\n\\nHowever, there have been legislative proposals to exempt overtime pay from federal income tax. The \\\"No Tax on Overtime\\\" proposal was part of broader tax reform discussions, but as of 2026, it has not become law.\\n\\nSome states may have different rules, so check with your state revenue department.\\n\\n## Are Taxes on Bonuses Higher?\\n\\nBonuses are subject to a **flat 22% federal withholding rate** (for bonuses under $1 million). This is often higher than your normal tax rate, which is why your bonus may seem \\\"taxed more.\\\"\\n\\nHowever, at tax time, your bonus is simply added to your total income and taxed at your marginal rate. If your marginal rate is lower than 22%, you'll get the excess withholding back as a refund.\\n\\n**For bonuses over $1 million**: The withholding rate jumps to 37%.\\n\\nUse our [Bonus Tax Calculator](/bonus-tax-calculator) to see exactly how much you'll keep.\\n\\n## Are Taxes Automatically Deducted from Paycheck?\\n\\nYes. Your employer withholds federal income tax, Social Security tax (6.2%), and Medicare tax (1.45%) from each paycheck. They also withhold state income tax (if applicable).\\n\\nThe amount withheld is based on:\\n- Your income\\n- Your filing status\\n- Your W-4 allowances\\n- Any additional withholding you requested\\n\\nUse our [Paycheck Calculator](/paycheck-calculator) to see your exact take-home pay.\\n\\n## Are Taxes Cheaper in Florida?\\n\\nYes. Florida has **no state income tax**, which means:\\n- No tax on wages, salaries, or self-employment income\\n- No tax on investment income\\n- No tax on pension or retirement income\\n- No tax on Social Security benefits\\n\\nOn a $100,000 salary, living in Florida instead of California saves approximately **$8,000-$10,000 per year** in state taxes.\\n\\nHowever, Florida does have:\\n- Sales tax: 6% (avg 6.98% with local surtaxes)\\n- Property tax: avg 0.98%\\n- Corporate income tax: 3.535%\\n\\n## Are Taxes Included in Mortgage?\\n\\nFor most homeowners, monthly mortgage payments include:\\n1. **Principal** (loan repayment)\\n2. **Interest** (cost of borrowing)\\n3. **Property taxes** (held in escrow)\\n4. **Homeowners insurance** (held in escrow)\\n5. **PMI** (if applicable)\\n\\nProperty taxes are typically collected by your lender and held in an escrow account, then paid to your local government when due. Not all mortgages include escrow — some homeowners pay property taxes directly.\\n\\nUse our [Mortgage Calculator](/mortgage-calculator) to see your full monthly payment including taxes.\\n\\n## Are Taxes Going Up in New York?\\n\\nNew York already has some of the highest state taxes in the country:\\n- State income tax: 4% - 10.9% (progressive)\\n- NYC resident tax: 3.078% - 3.876% (additional)\\n- Sales tax: avg 8.52%\\n- Property tax: avg 1.62%\\n\\nFor 2026, New York has not announced major tax increases, but the top marginal rate of 10.9% remains one of the highest in the nation. NYC residents pay the combined state + city rate.\\n\\n## Are Taxes Going Up in Virginia?\\n\\nVirginia's income tax rates range from 2% to 5.75%. For 2026, Virginia has not announced major rate increases, but standard deduction amounts and bracket thresholds are adjusted annually for inflation.\\n\\nVirginia does tax Social Security benefits for higher-income retirees, unlike many states that have exempted them.\\n\\n## Are Property Taxes Deductible?\\n\\nYes, but with limitations. As of 2026, you can deduct up to **$10,000** in combined state and local taxes (SALT) — this includes property taxes plus state income taxes OR state sales taxes.\\n\\nThis $10,000 cap (SALT cap) was introduced by the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017 and is scheduled to sunset after 2025. For 2026, the cap may be eliminated or modified.\\n\\n**To deduct property taxes**, you must itemize deductions (Schedule A) rather than take the standard deduction.\\n\\n## Are Tax Brackets Based on AGI?\\n\\nTax brackets are based on **taxable income**, which is your AGI minus either the standard deduction or itemized deductions.\\n\\nThe calculation:\\n1. Gross income (wages, self-employment, investments, etc.)\\n2. Minus adjustments (401k contributions, HSA, student loan interest) = **AGI**\\n3. Minus standard or itemized deductions = **Taxable income**\\n4. Tax brackets apply to taxable income\\n\\n## Are 1099 Taxes Quarterly?\\n\\nYes, if you're self-employed or receive 1099 income, you generally must pay **quarterly estimated taxes** if you expect to owe $1,000+ at tax time.\\n\\n### 2026 Quarterly Tax Due Dates\\n- Q1: April 15, 2026\\n- Q2: June 15, 2026\\n- Q3: September 15, 2026\\n- Q4: January 15, 2027\\n\\nUse our [Self-Employment Tax Calculator](/self-employment-tax-calculator) to estimate your quarterly payments.\\n\\n## Are Taxes Still Being Taken Out of Overtime Pay?\\n\\nYes. As of 2026, overtime pay is still subject to federal income tax withholding, Social Security tax, and Medicare tax — just like regular wages.\\n\\nYour employer withholds taxes from overtime pay at the same rate as regular pay. The \\\"No Tax on Overtime\\\" proposals have not been enacted into law.\\n\\nUse our [Overtime Tax Calculator](/overtime-tax-calculator) to see your after-tax overtime pay.\\n\\n## Related Resources\\n\\n- [Paycheck Calculator](/paycheck-calculator)\\n- [Bonus Tax Calculator](/bonus-tax-calculator)\\n- [Overtime Tax Calculator](/overtime-tax-calculator)\\n- [Self-Employment Tax Calculator](/self-employment-tax-calculator)\\n- [Property Tax Calculator](/property-tax-calculator)\\n- [Mortgage Calculator](/mortgage-calculator)\",\"new-york-tax-guide-2026\":\"# New York State Taxes 2026: NYC Tax, Filing, and What You Owe\\n\\nNew York has one of the most complex tax systems in the country. Between the state income tax, the NYC tax (for city residents), and various local taxes, understanding what you actually owe can feel like solving a puzzle. But once you break it down, the structure makes sense — even if the rates are painfully high.\\n\\nLet's walk through everything you need to know about New York state taxes in 2026, including the New York City income tax rate, NYC tax brackets, state income tax filing requirements, and how the tax year affects your bottom line.\\n\\n## New York State Income Tax Rates for 2026\\n\\nNew York uses a progressive income tax system with nine brackets for single filers, ranging from 4% to 10.9%. The rates are among the highest in the nation, especially for high earners.\\n\\n### 2026 New York State Tax Brackets (Single Filer)\\n\\n| Tax Rate | Taxable Income Range |\\n|----------|---------------------|\\n| 3.9% | $0 – $8,500 |\\n| 4.4% | $8,501 – $11,700 |\\n| 5.15% | $11,701 – $13,900 |\\n| 5.5% | $13,901 – $80,650 |\\n| 6% | $80,651 – $215,400 |\\n| 6.85% | $215,401 – $1,077,550 |\\n| 9.65% | $1,077,551 – $5,000,000 |\\n| 10.3% | $5,000,001 – $25,000,000 |\\n| 10.9% | Over $25,000,000 |\\n\\nFor most people earning between $14,000 and $80,000, the state income tax rate is 5.5%. On a $75,000 salary, you'll pay roughly $3,500 in New York state income tax — and that's before the NYC tax kicks in.\\n\\nUse our [New York Tax Calculator](/new-york-tax-calculator) to see your exact take-home pay.\\n\\n## NYC Tax: The City Income Tax Surcharge\\n\\nIf you live in New York City, you pay an additional city income tax on top of the state tax. The NYC tax is what makes New York uniquely expensive — no other city in America imposes an income tax this high.\\n\\n### 2026 NYC Tax Rates (Single Filer)\\n\\n| Tax Rate | Taxable Income Range |\\n|----------|---------------------|\\n| 3.078% | $0 – $12,000 |\\n| 3.762% | $12,001 – $25,000 |\\n| 3.819% | $25,001 – $50,000 |\\n| 3.876% | $50,001 – $500,000 |\\n| 3.876% | Over $500,000 |\\n\\n### Do You Pay NYC Tax If You Don't Live in NYC?\\n\\nNo. The NYC tax only applies to residents of the five boroughs (Manhattan, Brooklyn, Queens, The Bronx, Staten Island). If you live in Westchester, Long Island, or New Jersey but work in NYC, you do not pay the NYC tax — only the New York state income tax.\\n\\nHowever, if you're a part-year resident (you moved into or out of NYC during the tax year), you'll pay NYC tax on income earned while you were a resident.\\n\\n## York State Taxes: What Else You Need to Know\\n\\nBeyond the income tax, New York imposes several other taxes that affect residents:\\n\\n### Estate Tax\\n\\nNew York is one of the few states with its own estate tax. For 2026, the estate tax exemption is approximately $6.94 million. Estates valued above this threshold face a graduated rate from 3.06% to 16%. Importantly, New York's estate tax has a \\\"cliff\\\" provision — if your estate exceeds the exemption by even 5%, the entire amount becomes taxable, not just the excess.\\n\\n### Sales Tax\\n\\nNew York state sales tax is 4%, but local jurisdictions add significantly more:\\n- **NYC combined rate**: 8.875% (4% state + 4.5% city + 0.375% MCTD)\\n- **Most other areas**: 7% – 8.75%\\n\\n### Property Tax\\n\\nNew York's average effective property tax rate is about 1.62%, but it varies dramatically by location. Long Island has some of the highest property taxes in the nation, while some upstate areas are more moderate.\\n\\n## Filing Your New York Tax Returns\\n\\nNew York requires residents to file state tax returns if their federal adjusted gross income exceeds $4,000 (single) or the standard deduction amount for their filing status. Here's what you need for filing:\\n\\n### Required Forms\\n\\n1. **IT-201**: Resident Income Tax Return (most common)\\n2. **IT-203**: Nonresident/Part-Year Resident Return\\n3. **NYC-208**: NYC resident tax (if applicable)\\n\\n### Tax Year and Filing Deadlines\\n\\nThe tax year for New York follows the federal tax year — January 1 to December 31. Tax returns are due April 15 (or the next business day), same as federal returns.\\n\\nIf you need an extension, New York automatically grants a state extension if you file a federal extension. But remember — an extension to file is not an extension to pay. You must estimate and pay any taxes owed by the April deadline.\\n\\n### Tax Credits Available in New York\\n\\nNew York offers several tax credits that can reduce your state tax bill:\\n\\n- **Earned Income Tax Credit**: 30% of the federal EITC\\n- **Child and Dependent Care Credit**: Based on federal credit amount\\n- **Real Property Tax Credit**: For renters and homeowners with income under $18,000\\n- **College Tuition Credit**: Up to $400 per student\\n\\n## Personal Income and Your Total Tax Burden\\n\\nFor a single person earning $75,000 and living in NYC, the total personal income tax burden breaks down like this:\\n\\n- **Federal income tax**: ~$11,414\\n- **FICA (Social Security + Medicare)**: ~$5,738\\n- **New York state income tax**: ~$3,500\\n- **NYC income tax**: ~$2,500\\n- **Total income tax**: ~$23,152 (about 30.9% of gross income)\\n\\nThat's before sales tax, property tax, and other taxes. New York City residents have one of the highest total tax burdens in the United States.\\n\\n## Comparing New York to Other States\\n\\nThe tax difference between New York and no-income-tax states is dramatic:\\n\\n| Tax Type | New York (NYC) | Texas | Florida |\\n|----------|---------------|-------|---------|\\n| State Income Tax | 4% – 10.9% | 0% | 0% |\\n| City Income Tax | 3.078% – 3.876% | 0% | 0% |\\n| Sales Tax | 8.875% | 8.19% | 7.02% |\\n| Property Tax (avg) | 1.62% | 1.60% | 0.86% |\\n| Total Burden | ~12.7% | ~8.22% | ~6.97% |\\n\\nOn a $100,000 salary, living in NYC costs roughly $10,000–$12,000 more per year in taxes compared to Texas or Florida.
